Compute initial estimate via ridge regression

\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}

\displaystyle:=\textstyle\argmin_{A_{\mathrm{cl}}}\frac{1}{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}_{t+1}-A_{\mathrm{cl}}\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{\mathsf{safe}}}{2}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}

(b)

Refine estimate:
A_{0}=\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}-B_{0}K_{\mathsf{init}}
.

F.1
Incorporating Data Dependent Conditions for
\mathsf{WarmStart}

Let
P_{0}:=P_{\infty}(A_{0},B_{0})
denote the optimal value function of the initial estimate
(A_{0},B_{0})
. From our perturbation bounds on the solution to the DARE (
Proposition

C.3
), we have that if

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{op},0}:=\max\{\left\|A_{0}-A_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}},\left\|B_{0}-B_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\}\leq\eta/(16(1+\eta)^{4}\left\|P_{\infty}(A_{0},B_{0})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3},

for some parameter
\eta\in(0,1)
, then
\|P_{\star}-P_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\eta\|P_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. By choosing
\eta
sufficiently small, we can see that that if
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{op},0}\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}\|P_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3}}
, then the warm start condition
2.1
holds. Hence, by modifying constants, we can replace
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{stable}}
in the warm-start condition with a data-dependent condition
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{op},0}\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}\|P_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3}}
(for constant
c_{1}
), which depends only on the value function of the initial estimate. One can can show that this condition is guaranteed to be met as soon as
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{op},0}\leq\frac{1}{c_{2}\|P_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{3}}
, which means that the data-dependent warm start condition does not significantly alter what is required from the alignment condition.

F.2
Implementation via Representer Theorems

In the case where the states
\mathbf{x}_{t}
are infinite dimensional feature mappings equal to
\phi(\mathbf{y}_{t})
, where
\phi
is a kernel and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
is a finite dimensional observation, we can employ standard representer theorem arguments for kernel ridge regression in order to efficiently implement the algorithms above. More specifically, the estimates
\widehat{A}
and
\widehat{B}
can be represented via outer products of the data points
(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{v}_{t})
.

Furthermore, since inputs are finite dimensional, we can compute inverses and solve the Riccati equation by iterating the finite horizon version until convergence (see discussion in
Fazel et al. 2018
, Appendix A). That is, for
P_{1}=Q
,
Hewer 1971
shows that the following fixed point iteration is contractive and that
P_{\infty}(A,B)
is equal to the limit of,

P_{t+1}=Q+A^{\mathsf{H}}P_{t}A-A^{\mathsf{H}}P_{t}B(R+B^{\mathsf{H}}P_{t}B)^{-1}B^{\mathsf{H}}P_{t}A.

Having solved the Riccati equation, we can then compute controller by taking products of linear operators, for which we have tractable representations.

Appendix G
Regret Bounds

For the sake of the analysis in this section, we define the quantity
\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathcal{A},\mathbf{z})
as the regret incurred by the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
over
T
time steps starting from (a possibly random) initial state
\mathbf{z}
.

Lemma G.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm that chooses actions according to
\mathbf{u}_{t}=K\mathbf{x}_{t}+\mathbf{v}_{t}
for
\mathbf{v}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}I)
and let
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\mathbf{z}})
with
\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{z}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}~\leq~B_{\mathbf{z}}
. Then, with probability
1-\delta
,
\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathcal{A},\mathbf{z})
is less than or equal to,

\displaystyle 7\log_{+}(2/\delta)\left(T\left(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]\right)+B_{\mathbf{z}}\mathrm{tr}\left[P_{\infty}(K;A_{\star},B_{\star})\right]\right)-TJ_{\star},

where
Q_{0}:=Q+K^{\mathsf{H}}RK
and
A_{\mathrm{cl}}:=A_{\star}+B_{\star}K
.

Proof.

By definition, the regret of the algorithm is equal to:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{t},(Q+K^{\mathsf{H}}RK)\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\rangle+\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},R\mathbf{v}_{t}\right\rangle-TJ_{\star}.

(G.1)

The lemma follows from first showing that the relevant random variables concentrate around their expectations and then upper bounding these expectations.

Bounding exploration cost

Using the Hanson-Wright inequality (
Lemma

E.8
), we argue that with probability
1-\delta/2
, since
\mathbf{v}_{t}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}I)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle\mathbf{v}_{t},R\mathbf{v}_{t}\right\rangle^{2}=\|R^{1/2}\mathbf{v}_{t}\|^{2}~\leq~7\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}T\log_{+}(2/\delta)\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right].

More precisely, we have applied Hanson-Wright to the series of random variables
\tilde{\mathbf{v}}_{t}=R^{1/2}\mathbf{v}_{t}
and used the calculation,
\mathbb{E}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\mathbf{v}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{t}\right]=\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}
.

Bounding state cost

Now let
Q_{0}:=Q+K^{\mathsf{H}}RK
, we have that,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\langle\mathbf{x}_{t},(Q+K^{\mathsf{H}}RK)\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|Q_{0}^{1/2}\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}=\|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2},

where
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
is defined as,
\mathbf{z}_{0}:=\begin{bmatrix}Q_{0}^{1/2}\mathbf{x}_{1}$
\ldots$Q_{0}^{1/2}\mathbf{x}_{T}\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf{H}}
. Since all the
\mathbf{x}_{t}
are Gaussian,
\tilde{\mathbf{x}_{t}}
is also Gaussian, albeit in a different Hilbert space. Applying Hanson-Wright, we get that with probability
1-\delta/2
,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle~\leq~7\log_{+}(2/\delta)\mathbb{E}\mathrm{tr}\left[\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\right].

Bounding expectation

Letting
A_{\mathrm{cl}}:=A_{\star}+B_{\star}K
we have that by definition of the dynamical system, for
j~\geq~0
,

\mathbf{x}_{1+j}=A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{j}\mathbf{x}_{1}+\sum_{k=1}^{j}A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{k-1}(B_{\star}\mathbf{v}_{1+j-k}+\mathbf{w}_{1+j-k}).

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{1+j}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1+j}\right]\preceq A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{j}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1}\right]\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\;\mathsf{H}}\right)^{j}+\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right).

Using this, we can upper bound
\mathbb{E}\mathrm{tr}\left[\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\right]
as follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathrm{tr}\left[\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathbf{x}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle~\leq~\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{j}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1}\right]\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\;\mathsf{H}}\right)^{j}\right]+T\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]

(G.2)

\displaystyle~\leq~B_{\mathbf{z}}\mathrm{tr}\left[P_{\infty}(K;A_{\star},B_{\star})\right]+T\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left((A_{\star}+B_{\star}K)^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right].

(G.3)

The final inequality is justified by the following series of manipulations,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{j}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1}\right]\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\;\mathsf{H}}\right)^{j}\right]

\displaystyle~\leq~\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1}\right]\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\;\mathsf{H}}\right)^{j}(Q+K^{\mathsf{H}}PK)A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1}\right]\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[P_{\infty}(K;A_{\star},B_{\star})\right].

Wrapping up

Combining our results so far, we have that with probability
1-\delta
the regret as expressed in
Equation

G.1
is less than or equal to,

7\log_{+}(2/\delta)\left(T\left(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]\right)+B_{\mathbf{z}}\mathrm{tr}\left[P_{\infty}(K;A_{\star},B_{\star})\right]\right)-TJ_{\star}.

∎

G.1
Proof of
Corollary

3.1

Proof.

The proof of this proposition consists of a simple application of
Lemma

G.1
and then bounding the relevant terms to show that the regret is
\mathcal{O}(T_{\mathsf{init}})
. Recall that
\mathsf{WarmStart}
chooses inputs according to
\mathbf{u}_{t}=K_{\mathsf{init}}\mathbf{x}_{t}+\mathbf{v}_{t}
. Furthermore, the initial state is exactly 0 so
B_{\mathbf{z}}=0
in the statement of the lemma. Therefore, with probability
1-\delta
, for
A_{\mathrm{cl}}=A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{\mathsf{init}}
, the regret is smaller than:

\displaystyle 7T_{\mathsf{init}}\log_{+}(2/\delta)\left(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[(Q+K_{\mathsf{init}}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{\mathsf{init}})\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]\right)-T_{\mathsf{init}}J_{\star}.

We note that
\mathrm{tr}\left[(Q+K_{\mathsf{init}}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{\mathsf{init}})\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]~\leq~\left\|Q+K_{\mathsf{init}}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{\mathsf{init}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\star}(K_{\mathsf{init}},\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}})\right]
. Since
J_{\star}~\geq~0
, this expression above is then upper bounded by,

\displaystyle 7\log_{+}(2/\delta)\left(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+\left\|Q+K_{\mathsf{init}}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{\mathsf{init}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\star}(K_{\mathsf{init}},\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}})\right]\right)T_{\mathsf{init}}.

∎

G.2
Proof of
Theorem

3.1

Proof.

By definition of the algorithm in
Appendix

F
, we can split up the regret into two separate phases: an initial
\mathsf{explore}
phase and then a
\mathsf{commit}
phase.

\mathrm{Regret}_{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}(\mathsf{explore},\mathbf{x}_{1})+\mathrm{Regret}_{T-T_{\mathsf{exp}}}\left(\mathsf{commit},\mathbf{x}_{T_{\mathsf{exp}}+1}\right).

The
\mathsf{explore}
phase corresponds to the regret incurred during the first part of the algorithm wherein inputs are chosen according to
\mathbf{u}_{t}=K_{0}\mathbf{x}_{t}+\mathbf{v}_{t}
for
T_{\mathsf{exp}}
many iterations. The commit algorithm then chooses inputs
\mathbf{u}_{t}=\widehat{K}\mathbf{x}_{t}
for the remaining
T-T_{\mathsf{exp}}
time steps.

Exploration regret

We now upper bound the regret in each phase individually. For the exploration phase, we can apply
Lemma

G.1
, to get that with probability
1-\delta/4
, for
A_{\mathrm{cl}}=A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{0}
and
Q_{0}=Q+K_{0}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{0}
, the regret experienced in this phase bounded by,

\displaystyle 7\log_{+}(8/\delta)T_{\mathsf{exp}}\left(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]\right).

Since
\mathbf{x}_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})
is drawn from the same exploration distribution, the remaining constant term from
Lemma

G.1
vanishes since there is no need to consider the change in distribution. As in the proof of the
\mathsf{WarmStart}
regret bound, we observe that,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left[(Q+K_{0}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{0})\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}+B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\right)\right]~\leq~\left\|Q+K_{0}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right].

Given that the initial estimates satisfy
2.1
, by
Lemma

D.7
and
Lemma

C.8
we have that

\left\|K_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}~\leq~\left\|P_{\infty}(A_{0},B_{0})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\left\|Q+K_{0}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}~\leq~\left\|Q\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\|K_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|R\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim M_{\star}^{2}.

Therefore, with probability
1-\delta/4
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Regret}_{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}(\mathsf{explore},\mathbf{x}_{1})\lesssim\log_{+}(1/\delta)T_{\mathsf{exp}}\left(\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+M_{\star}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]\right).

(G.4)

Bounding regret during commit phase

Moving on to bounding regret during the second phase, our first observation is that due to the projection step, the system estimates
\widehat{A},\widehat{B}
lie inside an operator norm ball of radius
.5\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{stable}}
around the warm start estimates
(A_{0},B_{0})
. Since the warm start estimates are themselves
.5\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{stable}}
close to
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
, we conclude that
(\widehat{A},\widehat{B})
satisfy
2.1
and by
Lemma

C.8
,
\widehat{K}=K_{\infty}(\widehat{A},\widehat{B})
is guaranteed to be stabilizing for the true system
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
. Furthermore,
\left\|P_{0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim M_{\star}
and
\|\widehat{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim M_{\star}
.

Next, we use a similar regret analysis as before and apply
Lemma

G.1
, to conclude that with probability
1-\delta/4
,
\mathrm{Regret}_{T-T_{\mathsf{exp}}}\left(\mathsf{commit},\mathbf{x}_{T_{\mathsf{exp}}+1}\right)
is upper bounded by,

\displaystyle 7T\log_{+}(8/\delta)\left(\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right)\right]-\mathcal{J}_{\star}\right)+7\log_{+}(8/\delta)\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[P_{\infty}(K_{0};A_{\star},B_{\star})\right],

(G.5)

where
A_{\mathrm{cl}}=A_{\star}+B_{\star}\widehat{K}
and
Q_{0}=Q+\widehat{K}^{\mathsf{H}}R\widehat{K}
. Next, since

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left[Q_{0}\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}}^{\mathsf{H}},\;\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right)\right]=\mathrm{tr}\left[(Q+\widehat{K}^{\mathsf{H}}R\widehat{K})\Sigma_{\star}(\widehat{K})\right]=\mathcal{J}(\widehat{K}),

we can rewrite
Equation

G.5
as,

7T\log_{+}(8/\delta)(J(\widehat{K})-J(K_{\star}))+7\log_{+}(8/\delta)\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[P_{\infty}(K_{0};A_{\star},B_{\star})\right].

Using
Theorem

2.1
,

\mathcal{J}(\widehat{K})-\mathcal{J}(K_{\star})\lesssim M_{\star}^{36}\cdot\mathcal{L}\exp(\smash{\tfrac{1}{50}}\sqrt{\mathcal{L}})\cdot\varepsilon^{2},\quad\text{ where }\mathcal{L}:=\log\left(e+\smash{\tfrac{2e\|\widehat{A}-A_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\right),

where
\varepsilon=\max\left\{\left\lVert(\widehat{A}-A_{\star})\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}},\left\lVert\widehat{B}-B_{\star}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}\right\}
and
\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}
has been set to 1 as in the description of the
\mathsf{OnlineCE}
in
Appendix

F
. To finish the proof, we now plug in our estimation rates from
Part

II
to upper bound
\varepsilon
and optimize over
T_{\mathsf{exp}}
.

Plugging in estimation rates

Using
Proposition

E.2
, for
T_{\mathsf{exp}}\gtrsim d_{u}\log\left(\frac{d_{u}}{\delta}\right)
, with probability
1-\delta/4
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert B_{\star}-\widehat{B}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{d_{u}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]\log\left(\frac{d_{u}}{\delta}\right)}{\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}T_{\mathsf{exp}}}.

Incorporating the analogous proposition for
A_{\star}
,
Proposition

E.3
, for

T_{\mathsf{exp}}\gtrsim d_{\lambda}\log_{+}\left(\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]}{\delta\lambda}\right)+\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log_{+}\left(\frac{\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{\lambda}\left\|B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}+\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right),

with probability
1-\delta/4

\left\lVert(\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}-A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}})\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}\lesssim\lambda\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}+\cdot\frac{W_{\mathrm{tr}}}{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})\log_{+}\left(\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]}{\delta\lambda}\right).

Setting
\lambda=c\frac{W_{\mathrm{tr}}}{T_{\mathsf{exp}}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}}
for some universal constant
c
, the second term dominates the first and we get that,

\left\lVert(\widehat{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}-A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}})\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{W_{\mathrm{tr}}}{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]T_{\mathsf{exp}}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}}{W_{\mathrm{tr}}\delta}\right).

From our definition of
\varepsilon
, we see that it is upper bounded by, the sum of the errors in
A_{\star}
and
B_{\star}
and hence,

\varepsilon^{2}\lesssim\frac{d_{u}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]+W_{\mathrm{tr}}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})}{T_{\mathsf{exp}}}\log\left(\frac{d_{u}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}T}{W_{\mathrm{tr}}\delta^{2}}\right),

where above we also upper bounded
T_{\mathsf{exp}}\leq T
.

Wrapping up

All that remains is to optimize over
T_{\mathsf{exp}}
to balance the regret between both phases. In particular, if we choose,

\displaystyle T_{\mathsf{exp}}=\sqrt{\frac{T\cdot M_{\star}^{36}\left(d_{u}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]+W_{\mathrm{tr}}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})\right)}{\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+M_{\star}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]}},

we get that with probability
1-\delta
, up to constants and log factors, the total regret is bounded by:

\displaystyle\sqrt{(\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+M_{\star}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right])M_{\star}^{36}\left(d_{u}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]+W_{\mathrm{tr}}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})\right)T}\cdot\varphi(T)

where
\varphi(T)=\exp\left(\sqrt{\log\left(1+\sqrt{T}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]\right)}\right)
. Simplifying the bound a bit further, we know that
\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]~\leq~\left\|R\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}d_{u}
. And, by
Lemma

D.8
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\left[\mathsf{dlyap}\left((A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{0})^{\mathsf{H}},\;B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}+\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle~\leq~\left\|\mathsf{dlyap}\left((A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{0})^{\mathsf{H}},\;Q+K_{0}^{\mathsf{H}}RK_{0}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\mathrm{tr}\left[B_{\star}B_{\star}^{\mathsf{H}}\sigma^{2}_{\mathbf{u}}+\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right]

\displaystyle~\leq~M_{\star}(M_{\star}+\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right])

\displaystyle\lesssim M_{\star}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right].

Hence, if we let
d_{\max}:=\max\{\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right],W_{\mathrm{tr}},d_{u}\}
we get that:

\mathrm{tr}\left[R\right]+M_{\star}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]\lesssim M_{\star}^{4}d_{\max}\text{ and }d_{u}\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right]+W_{\mathrm{tr}}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})\lesssim M_{\star}^{2}d_{\max}^{2}.

Therefore, we can upper bound the total regret of
\mathsf{OnlineCE}
is with high probability
\mathcal{O}_{\star}\left(\sqrt{M_{\star}^{42}d_{\max}^{2}(d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda})T}\right)
.
∎

G.3
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

Proof.

The proof of the theorem follows by bounding
d_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}
based on the decay rates of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}
. We overload notation and define
d_{\lambda}(\Lambda)
for
\Lambda\succeq 0
to be the number of eigenvalues of
\Lambda
that are larger than
\lambda
. Likewise, we define
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}(\Lambda)
to be the sum of the eigenvalues of
\Lambda
that are smaller than
\lambda
, divided by
\lambda
. The proof follows by applying two inequalities which follow from
Lemma

D.15
. In particular, since
B_{\star}
is finite rank and by the warm start property, the following are true for
n\gtrsim n_{0}:=\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log\left(\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}W_{\mathrm{tr}}/\lambda\right)
,

\displaystyle d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})

\displaystyle~\leq~n_{0}d_{\lambda/(2\|P_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2})}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}})+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})

\displaystyle~\leq~\frac{1}{\lambda}n_{0}\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left(\sum_{j~\geq~\lceil a\rceil}^{\infty}\sigma_{j}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}})+\lambda\right)+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}},

where
a=d_{\lambda}\left(\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right)/n_{0}
.
We analyze each case separately.

Polynomial decay

If
\sigma_{j}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}})=j^{-\alpha}
, then a short calculation shows that
d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}})=\lfloor\lambda^{-1/\alpha}\rfloor
. Therefore,

d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})~\leq~n_{0}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}\right)^{-1/\alpha}+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Since
\lambda=c\cdot\frac{W_{\mathrm{tr}}}{T\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}}
for some constant
c
,
d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})
scales no faster than
n_{0}T^{1/\alpha}+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. For
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}
, we have that

\sum_{j~\geq~\lceil\frac{\lambda^{-1/\alpha}}{n_{0}}\rceil}j^{-\alpha}~\leq~\int_{\frac{\lambda^{-1/\alpha}}{n_{0}}}j^{-\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\lambda^{(\alpha-1)/\alpha}n_{0}^{\alpha-1}.

Therefore, after dividing by
\lambda
, we get that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}
scales as
n_{0}\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}T^{1/\alpha}\left\lVert A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. Since
\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim M_{\star}^{3}
, we get that
d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}
are
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(M_{\star}^{4}T^{1/\alpha})
.

Exponential decay

Moving on to the case where the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}
decay exponentially fast, a similar calculation to the previous one shows that
d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}})=\lfloor\alpha^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)\rfloor
. Therefore,

\displaystyle d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})\lesssim\frac{n_{0}}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{\lambda}\right)+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Hence
d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})
scales as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(n_{0}+d_{u}M_{\star}^{3})
. For the tail term, we observe that
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\exp(-\alpha j)=1/(\exp(\alpha)-1)
. This term therefore scales no faster than
n_{0}\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}+d_{u}\left\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. This shows that
d_{\lambda}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}
are
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(M_{\star}^{3}d_{u})
.

Finite dimension

In finite dimension with full rank noise, it is clear that for large enough
T
,
d_{\lambda}(\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})
is bounded by
d_{x}
and that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathsf{tail},\lambda}
is equal to 0. Furthermore, using now standard analysis such as the ones present in
Simchowitz et al. 2018
, it follows that
\|\widehat{A}-A_{\star}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}
goes to 0 at the same rate as
\|(\widehat{A}-A_{\star})\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}^{1/2}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}
. Therefore, the terms depending on
\mathcal{L}
in
Theorem

3.1
become
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

∎

G.4
Combining WarmStart and OnlineCE

In the analysis of
\mathsf{OnlineCE}
(
Theorem

3.1
), we assumed that
\mathbf{x}_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0})
was distributed according to the steady state distribution of induced by the exploration policy. This assumption can be relaxed, since any initial distribution over
\mathbf{x}_{1}
will converge exponentially quickly to the steady steady in Wasserstein distance due to a mixing argument (variants of this argument are ubiquitous in the analysis of online LQR, and for brevity we omit them. The curious reader can see appendices of
Dean et al. 2018
,
Abeille and Lazaric 2020
for examples. The mixing time will be a polynomial in
\|P_{\infty}(K_{0};A_{\star},B_{\star})\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, which we show is
\lesssim\left\|P_{\star}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
.

Hence to stitch the two regret bounds together, we simply run the initial phase to garner estimates
(A_{0},B_{0})
, begin to play controller
K_{0}=K_{\infty}(A_{0},B_{0},
), allow a constant-length burnin for the state to converge to the distribution of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{x},0}
, and then execute
\mathsf{OnlineCE}
. Again, for the sake of brevity, we omit the details.

As a final remark, when stitching both algorithms together, one could in principle omit synthesizing the controller
K_{0}
as outlined in the first step of the
\mathsf{OnlineCE}
algorithm and run the entire exploration phase just using
K_{\mathsf{init}}
. Doing so would only increase the constants
M_{\star}
since they would now depend on
\left\|P_{\infty}(K_{\mathsf{init}};A_{\star},B_{\star})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. The asymptotics of the algorithm would remain unchanged. However, the projection step onto a safe set around
(A_{0},B_{0})
is crucial for our analysis in order to ensure that the certainty equivalent controller is stabilizing for the true system.

Appendix H
Lower Bound

In this section, we state and prove lower bounds demonstrating the necessity of finite input dimension; these results follow from applications of the lower bound due to
Simchowitz and Foster 2020
. Our first bound is as follows:

Theorem

1.1
.

Let
c,c^{\prime}>0
denote universal constants. Fix any trace bound
\gamma\geq 1
and input dimension
d_{u}\in\mathbb{N}
with
d_{u}\geq\sqrt{\log(1+\gamma)}
. Consider the set
\mathcal{U}
of instances with state dimension
d_{x}=\lfloor\gamma\rfloor
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:=\{(A,B):=\|A-\frac{1}{2}I\|_{\mathsf{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad\|B\|_{\mathsf{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\}.

Then, the LQR regret with cost matrices
Q=I_{d_{x}}
,
R=I_{d_{u}}
, and noise
\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=1
,
\mathrm{tr}[\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}]\leq\gamma
satisfies

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{A,{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq c\cdot\begin{cases}T&T\in[c^{\prime}\gamma\log(1+\gamma),\gamma d_{u}^{2}]\\
\sqrt{\gamma d_{u}^{2}\cdot T}&T\geq\gamma d_{u}^{2}\end{cases}.

We prove the bound in the following subsection. The bound considers instances that lie in a finite dimensional Hilbert space of dimension
d_{x}=\lfloor\gamma\rfloor
. In particular, all the instances in the packing are operator norm bounded, Hilbert-Schmidt, and in fact finite rank. The difficulty introduced by high-dimensional inputs is one of a needle in the haystack: to find the optimal control policy, the learner needs to learn to align their controller with the true
B_{\star}
matrix, and doing so incurs dependence on ambient dimension. In essence, this is because the learner is free to pick any direction she chooses, so the complexity of the problem behaves more like, say, a linear bandit problem in dimension
d_{u}
than a statistical learning problem which admits a more refined notion of intrinsic dimension.

In
Section

H.2
, we state and sketch the proof of a lower bound that holds
even if
all the instances are controllable, demonstrating that little can be done to remove the finite dimensionality requirement of inputs.

Remark H.1

(Local Mimimax Bounds)
.

The bound stated above considers the minimax regret over a ball of constant radius. In contrast, the lower bound for finite dimensional online LQR due to
Simchowitz and Foster 2020
is stronger in the following ways: (a) it considers
local minimax
regret over alternatives with a vanishingly small radius (
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{T})
) of a nominal instance
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
and (b) it establishes lower bounds for
any
sufficiently nondegenerate
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
.

Since our lower bound builds on theirs, we can in fact strengthen
Theorem

1.1
to hold for a set
\mathcal{U}
contained in a ball of radius
\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{T})
, thereby accomplishing point (a). Regarding point (b): we chose to state our lower bound for a fixed instance to clarify the dependence on dimension without requiring further dependence on problem parameters. In principle, similar guarantees can be established for perturbations of arbitrary instances
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
, but these would require additional work to clarify both (i) the norm of the value function for such an instance, and (ii) the singular values
\sigma_{m}(A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{\star})
of the optimal solution to the closed loop matrix (in view of
Simchowitz and Foster 2020
). By addressing both terms, one could show, for example, that even in vanishly small neighborhoods of Hilbert-Schmidt, but still infinite dimensional
A_{\star}
, the
d_{u}
-term in the regret is unavoidable. In the interest of brevity, we leave these calculations to future work.

H.1
Proof of
Theorem

1.1

Throughout, we let
c_{i},i>1
denote universal constants. Recall that
\gamma\geq 1
is the trace bound, and
d_{x}=\lfloor\gamma\rfloor
is the state dimension. We select
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}=I_{d_{x}}
, which has trace
\mathrm{tr}\left[\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\right]=d_{x}\leq\gamma
.

Our lower bound follows from specializing the lower bound due to
Simchowitz and Foster 2020
. To begin, we state a variant of their main lower bound.

Proposition H.1

(Variant of Theorem 1 in
Simchowitz and Foster 2020
)
.

Let
c_{1},c_{2},p>0
denote universal constants. Consider a finite dimensional LQR system
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
, with finite input dimension
d_{u}
, state dimension
d_{x}
, cost matrices
R,Q\succeq I
, optimal controller
K_{\star}
, value function
P_{\star}
, and noise
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}=I_{d_{x}}
. Suppose
\nu:=\sigma_{\min}(A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{\star})/\|R+B_{\star}^{\top}P_{\star}B_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>0
. Then, defining the convex set,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}:=\{(A,B):\|A-A_{\star}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}+\|B-B_{\star}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}\}\leq\frac{1}{16},

(H.1)

it holds that,

\displaystyle\min_{\mathsf{alg}}\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{E}_{A,B}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq c_{2}\sqrt{d_{x}d_{u}^{2}T}\cdot\frac{\min\{1,\nu^{2}\}}{\|P_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}},

provided that
T\geq c_{1}\|P_{\star}\|^{p}_{\mathrm{op}}\min\{d_{u}^{2}d_{x},\frac{d_{x}\max\{1,\nu^{4}\}\max\{1,\|B_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4}\}}{d_{u}^{2}},d_{x}\log(1+d_{x}\|P_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}})\}
.

Proof.

Proposition

H.1
is obtained by specializing the proof of the lower bound from Theorem 1 in
Simchowitz and Foster 2020
to
m=d_{u}
, and noting that the instances in the construction of the lower bound lie in an ball which satisfies the conditions of Lemma 4.1 their work (note that, by enlarging the constant term in the unspecified polynomial in that lemma, we can ensure that the constant is sufficiently small).
∎

First, for simplicity, we specialize
Proposition

H.1
with a concrete instance. Take
A_{\star}=\frac{1}{2}I
,
R=Q=I
and let
B_{\star}=\mathbf{0}
.

Lemma H.2
.

Let
P_{\star}
denote the value function for the instance
(A_{\star},B_{\star})
,
K_{\star}
the optimal controller, and
A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}:=A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{\star}
the optimal closed loop systems. Then,
P_{\star}=\frac{4}{3}I
,
K_{\star}=0
, and
A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}=A_{\star}=\frac{1}{2}I
.

Proof.

Since
B_{\star}=\mathbf{0}
,
A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}}=A_{\star}
. Moreover, zero
B_{\star}
means that the control inputs do not affect the system. Hence, optimal performance is optimized by selecting no control input, so as to minimize input cost; that is,
K_{\star}=0
. Hence, the value function is
\mathsf{dlyap}\left(A_{\mathrm{cl}_{\star}},\;Q\right)=\sum_{j\geq 0}(\frac{1}{2}I)^{j}I(\frac{1}{2})^{j}=I\cdot\sum_{j\geq 0}4^{-j}=\frac{1}{1-1/4}I=\frac{4}{3}I
.
∎

Invoking
Lemma

H.4
, and using
d_{u}^{2}\geq\log(1+\gamma)\geq\log(1+d_{x})
to simplify terms, we find that for universal (i.e. dimension independent) constants
c_{3},c_{4}
, the instances centered in
\mathcal{B}:=\{(A,B):\|A-A_{\star}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}+\|B\|_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}\}\leq\frac{1}{4}\}
satisfy

\displaystyle\min_{\mathsf{alg}}\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{B}}\mathbb{E}_{A,B}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq c_{3}\sqrt{d_{x}d_{u}^{2}\cdot T},\quad\forall T\geq c_{4}d_{u}^{2}d_{x}.

(H.2)

To conclude, we address the case where
T\leq c_{4}d_{u}^{2}d_{x}
. For
T\geq 4c_{4}d_{x}\log(1+d_{x})
, consider a smaller input dimension
d:=\sqrt{\lfloor T/c_{4}d_{x}\rfloor}
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d}:=\{(A,\tilde{B})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}^{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}d}:\|A-A_{\star}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}+\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}^{2}\}\leq\frac{1}{4}\}
denote the analogous set of local instances to the above construction with input dimension
d
. From the choice of
d
and condition on
T
, we have
d^{2}\geq\log(1+d_{x})
and
T\geq c_{4}d^{2}d_{u}
, so the above lower bound established in dimension
d_{u}
entails that, for another universal constant
c_{5}
,

\displaystyle\min_{\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d}}\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\tilde{\mathsf{alg}})]\geq c_{3}\sqrt{d_{x}d^{2}\cdot T}\geq c_{5}T,

where the last inequality follows from the choice of
d:=\sqrt{\lfloor T/c_{4}d_{x}\rfloor}
. Stated otherwise, it holds that for all
T\in[4c_{4}d_{x}\log(1+d_{x}),c_{4}d_{x}d_{u}^{2}]
,

\displaystyle\exists\;d\in[1,d_{u}]\text{ such that }\min_{\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d}}\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\tilde{\mathsf{alg}})]\geq c_{5}T.

We now embed the above instances of input dimension
d
into input dimension
d_{u}
via the following lemma.

Lemma H.3
.

Fix a state dimension
d_{x}
, and an input dimension
d\leq d_{u}
. Given a matrix
\tilde{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}\times d}
, let
\iota(\tilde{B})
denote its canonical embedding into
\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}\times d_{u}}
by padding the remaining
d_{u}-d
columns with zeros. Overloading notation, given a subset of
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}^{2}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}d_{u}}
, define its embedding

\iota(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}):=\{(A,B):~\exists(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\text{ with }B=\iota(\tilde{B})\}.

Then,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathsf{alg}}\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{A,B,}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq\inf_{\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\tilde{\mathsf{alg}})],

where on both sides, the noise covariance is
\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}=I
and the state cost
Q=I_{d_{x}}
. On the left hand side, the input cost is
I_{d_{u}}
, and on the right,
R=I_{d}
.

Thus, letting
\bar{\mathcal{B}}:=\bigcup_{d\in[1,d_{u}]}\iota(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d})
denote the union of all the embeddings of the sets
\bar{\mathcal{B}}
, it holds that for all
T\in[4c_{4}d_{x}\log(1+d_{x}),c_{4}d_{x}d_{u}^{2}]
,

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{((A,B)\in\bar{\mathcal{B}}}\mathbb{E}_{A,{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq c_{5}T.

Moreover, since
\bar{\mathcal{B}}\supset\mathcal{B}
, incorporating
Equation

H.2
we conclude that,

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{((A,B)\in\bar{\mathcal{B}}}\mathbb{E}_{A,{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq\begin{cases}c_{5}T&T\in[4c_{4}d_{x}\log(1+d_{x}),c_{4}d_{x}d_{u}^{2}]\\
c_{3}\sqrt{d_{x}d_{u}^{2}\cdot T}&T\geq c_{4}d_{x}d_{u}^{2}\end{cases}.

Replacing
d_{x}
with
\lfloor\gamma\rfloor
, absorbing constants, and simplifying, we find that for universal constants
c_{6},c_{7}
:

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{((A,B)\in\bar{\mathcal{B}}}\mathbb{E}_{A,{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq c_{6}\begin{cases}T&T\in[c_{7}\gamma\log(1+\gamma),\gamma d_{u}^{2}]\\
\sqrt{\gamma d_{u}^{2}\cdot T}&T\geq\gamma d_{u}^{2}\end{cases}.

Finally, we observe that

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{B}}\subset\mathcal{U}:=\{(A,B):=\|A-\frac{1}{2}I\|_{\mathsf{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad\|B\|_{\mathsf{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\}.

Proof of
Lemma

H.3
.

We begin with the following claim.

Claim H.1
.

Let
\mathsf{alg}
be an algorithm which interacts with instances
(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}
. Then, there is an algorithm
\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}
which interacts with instances
(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
for which,

\displaystyle\forall A,\tilde{B},B=\iota(\tilde{B}),\quad\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B},\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,B,\mathsf{alg}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2}],

with equality if
\mathsf{alg}
always plays inputs
\mathbf{u}_{t}
whose last
d_{u}-d
coordinates are
0
.

Proof.

Given an input
\mathbf{u}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{u}}
, write
\mathbf{u}_{t}=(\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t},\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{t})
as the decomposition of
\mathbf{u}_{t}
into its first
d
, and last
d_{u}-d
coordinates. Observe that, for instances
(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}
, the last
d_{u}-d
coordinates
\mathring{\mathbf{u}}_{t}

do not
affect the dynamics. Hence, the iterates
(\mathbf{x}_{t},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t})
produced by
\mathsf{alg}
on
(A,B)
in
\mathcal{U}
coincide with the iterates obtained by the algorithm
\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}
which, given an instances
(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
proceeds as follows:

•

\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}
maintains “internal” inputs
\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
corresponding to the inputs that would have been selected by the original algorithm
\mathsf{alg}
with input dimension
d_{u}
.

•

For each
t
,
\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}
feeds
\mathsf{alg}
the past iterates
\mathbf{x}_{1:t},\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{1:t-1}
, and receive internal input
\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
.

•

Then,
\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}
plays the input
\mathbf{u}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
obtained by projecting
\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
onto its first
d
coordinates.

Given an instance
(A,\tilde{B})\in\tilde{U}
, and its embedding
(A,B)=(A,\iota(\tilde{B}))
, the iterates
(\mathbf{x}_{t},\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t})
produced by
\mathsf{alg}
on
(A,B)
have the same distribution as the iterates
(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{u}_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
produced by
\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}
. Hence, for all
A,\tilde{B},B=\iota(\tilde{B})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B},\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,B,\mathsf{alg}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{t}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,B,\mathsf{alg}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2}],

with equality if remaining
d_{u}-d
coordinates of the inputs prescribed by
\mathsf{alg}
are identically
0
for all
t
.
∎

Arguing along the lines of
H.1
, we can also see that the optimal infinite horizon control policy for
(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}
also only selects inputs supported on the first
d
coordinates, and thus

\displaystyle J^{\star}_{A,B}=J^{\star}_{A,\tilde{B}},\quad\forall(A,\tilde{B})\in\mathcal{U},B=\iota(\tilde{B}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{A,B}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]

\displaystyle=\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{A,B,\mathsf{alg}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2}]-TJ^{\star}_{A,B}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B},\tilde{\mathsf{alg}}}[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{2}]-TJ^{\star}_{A,\tilde{B}},\quad\text{ where }B=\iota(\tilde{B})

\displaystyle\geq\max_{(A,B)\in\tilde{\mathcal{U}}}\mathbb{E}_{A,\tilde{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\tilde{\mathsf{alg}})],

as needed.
∎

H.2
Lower Bound that Maintains Controllability

In this section, we state a lower bound that maintains controllability, in order to demonstrate how controllability does not ameliorate the requirement that the input dimension
d_{u}
be bounded. To capture this scenario, set

\displaystyle d_{x}\leq d_{u},\quad A_{\star}=\frac{1}{2}I,\quad B_{\star}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{d_{x}}&0_{d_{x}}\end{bmatrix}.

(H.3)

Theorem H.1
.

Let
c,c^{\prime}
be universal constants. Let
c,c^{\prime}>0
denote universal constants. Fix any trace bound
\gamma\geq 1
and input dimension
d_{u}\in\mathbb{N}
with
d_{u}\geq\gamma
. Consider the set
\mathcal{U}
of instances with state dimension
d_{x}=\lfloor\gamma\rfloor
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:=\left\{(A,B):=\|A-\frac{1}{2}I\|_{\mathsf{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{4},\quad\|B-B_{\star}\|_{\mathsf{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\right\},\quad\text{where }B_{\star}\text{ is in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:bst}.}

Then, the LQR regret with cost matrices
Q=I_{d_{x}}
,
R=I_{d_{u}}
,
\|\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=1
, and
\mathrm{tr}[\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}}]\leq\gamma
, satisfies,

\displaystyle\min_{{\mathsf{alg}}}\max_{(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{A,{B}}[\mathrm{Regret}_{T}(\mathsf{alg})]\geq\sqrt{\gamma d_{u}^{2}\cdot T},

for all
T\geq c^{\prime}\gamma d_{u}^{2}
. In particular, if
T\propto\gamma d_{u}^{2}
, the minimax regret on
\mathcal{U}
is linear in
T
.

Note that, for all instances
(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}
, not only is
A
stable (
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 3/4
), but the column space has rank
d_{x}
, and smallest singular value at least
3/4
(since the first
d_{x}
columns of
B_{\star}
are the identity, and all matrices are in
\mathcal{U}
are a bounded perturbation thereof). Hence, the systems
(A,B)\in\mathcal{U}
are all one-step controllable. Nevertheless, the regret still scales with
d_{u}^{2}
.

The proof of
Theorem

H.1
is nearly the same as that of
Theorem

1.1
; the main difference is verifying the bounds on
P_{\star}
and
\sigma_{\min}
required to instantiate
Proposition

H.1
.

Lemma H.4
.

Regardless of the choice of dimension, we have that
\|P_{\star}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 4/3
, and
\sigma_{\min}(A_{\star}+B_{\star}K_{\star})\geq 1/5
.

Proof of
Lemma

H.4
.

For simplicity, we drop the stars in the subscript. Let us characterize the optimal solution. Define
\mathcal{F}(P):=A^{\top}PA-(A^{\top}PB)(R+B^{\top}PB)^{-1}(B^{\top}PA)+Q-P
. Let
J
denote the projection onto the subspace spanned by the columns of
B
, and
J_{\perp}=I-J
. We guess a solution of the form

\displaystyle P=p_{1}J+p_{2}J_{\perp}.

Next, we show that such a
P
solves
\mathcal{F}(P)=0
when
p_{1},p_{2}
are appropriately chosen. With this matrix,
R+B^{\top}PB=R+p_{1}I_{d_{u}}=(1+p)I_{d_{u}}
, so
B(R+B^{\top}PB)^{-1}B^{\top}=(1+p_{1})J
, where
J
is the projection onto the subspace spanned by the columns of
B
. Let
J^{\perp}=I-J
. Note that in the overactuated case,
J
is the identity. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(P)

\displaystyle=\frac{P}{4}-\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{4(1+p_{1})}J-P+I

\displaystyle=(1+\frac{p_{1}}{4}-p_{1}-\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{4(1+p_{1})})J+(1+\frac{p_{2}}{4}-p_{2})J_{\perp}

\displaystyle=(1-\frac{3p_{1}}{4}-\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{4(1+p_{1})})J+(1+\frac{-3p_{2}}{4})J_{\perp}.

To solve this equation, set
p_{2}=\frac{4}{3}
, and set

\displaystyle 1-\frac{3p_{1}}{4}-\frac{p_{1}^{2}}{4(1+p_{1})}=0

\displaystyle-4(1+p_{1})+3p_{1}(1+p_{1})+p_{1}^{2}=0

\displaystyle 4p_{1}^{2}-p_{1}-4=0.

Taking the positive solution of the quadratic equation,
p_{1}=\frac{1+\sqrt{65}}{8}\leq 4/3
.

Now, the optimal control policy is
K=-(R+B^{\top}PB)^{-1}B^{\top}PA=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p_{1}}{1+p_{1}}B^{\top}
(using the form of
B
,
R=I
, and
P
), yielding
BK=-\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\frac{p_{1}}{1+p_{2}})J
. Hence,
A+BK=\frac{1}{2}\left(J_{\perp}+\frac{1}{1+p_{2}}J\right)
, and thus,
\sigma_{\min}(A+BK)\geq\frac{1}{2(1+p_{2})}\geq 1/5
.
∎
